[Director 指令] [Patch — Reviewer 最终验证后微调，4 项 info 级]

**1. p < 10^{-6} 精度修正**
W7 20-seed Wilcoxon 最小 p = 1.91e-6 > 1e-6。
- Abstract 和 Introduction 中的 "p < 10^{-6}" → "p < 0.001"（更安全，科学结论不变）

**2. ESS 最小值核实**
experiments.tex 报 "minimum bulk ESS of 217"，但 registry convergence.ess_min = 174（可能来自 item b_i 参数）。
- 如果 174 是所有参数的真实最小值 → 改为 174
- 或者限定 "minimum bulk ESS of 217 for model-level parameters (θ, c_n, d_j)"
- 无论哪个值都满足 ESS > 100 阈值

**3. N ≥ 30 vs N ≈ 17 协调**
Conclusion 推荐 "N ≥ 30"，Limitations 给出 "N ≈ 17 for 80% power"。
- Conclusion 改为 "N ≥ 20" 或加限定词："N ≥ 30 for well-powered detection of moderate effects"
- 或者 Limitations 处补充 "N ≈ 17 suffices for 80% power at the observed effect size; N ≥ 30 provides >95% power"

**4. 编译确认 8 页合规**
内容扩展较多，请编译后确认正文（§1-§6 Conclusion 末尾）不超过 page 8。如果超出，优先压缩 §4.2 MCMC 讨论和 §5 Related Work。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UFcZqtHWx1V4GkjpeQWv3K`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced G-MFRM, which jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility by formalizing their confounding as omitted variable bias. On Arena-Hard, G-MFRM produces the first joint contamination--severity diagnostic, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$; not significant at $N = 8$). The framework requires a multi-judge panel and externally estimated per-item exposure---conditions met by standard LLM evaluation pipelines but not by single-judge or closed benchmarks. Validation on larger model populations ($N \geq 30$) would improve statistical power for ranking differences. More broadly, treating judge bias and contamination as separable leads to systematic estimation errors; future LLM evaluation frameworks should model these facets jointly.
5	
```

</details>

On Arena-Hard, G-MFRM produces the first joint contamination--severity diagnostic, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; not significant at $N = 8$). The framework requires a multi-judge panel and externally estimated per-item exposure---conditions met by standard LLM evaluation pipelines but not by single-judge or closed benchmarks. Validation on larger model populations ($N \\geq 30$) would improve statistical power for ranking differences. More broadly, treating judge bias and contamination as separable leads to systematic estimation errors; future LLM evaluation frameworks should model these facets jointly.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 5,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 5
    }
  },
```

### Tool result

_source line 3061 · 2026-05-23T02:49:14.115Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019KxLKVLzBn7d3sZyWbQCqA`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}} = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 10^{-6}$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (top-4 models' 95\% CIs exclude zero, though posterior CI coverage is 71\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation provides decisive model preference for G-MFRM over standard MFRM ($\Delta\mathrm{ELPD} = -60$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$).
3	\end{abstract}
```

</details>

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for full coverage across all judges and target models, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (5{,}661 observations; 44.7\\% fill rate of the full $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Eight target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score all model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro NUTS with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ and bulk/tail ESS \\citep{vehtari2021rhat}.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nRaw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nMFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nSequential & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nDCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nPref.\\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\n\\midrule\nG-MFRM (ours) & \\textbf{0.857} & \\textbf{0.952} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (8 target models, 9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\\tau$ takes only ${\\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$, $\\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\\Delta\\tau \\geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\\theta_{\\text{mini}} > \\theta_{\\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$, $\\theta$-difference 95\\% CI $[-0.227, +0.722]$).\n\nWhile the bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides decisive evidence that the contamination gating term captures genuine data structure: G-MFRM achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$, far exceeding the threshold of 2.0 for decisive model preference; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. The contamination parameters improve out-of-sample prediction, not merely in-sample fit.\n\nThe MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 217. These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\\sigma$ remains below $\\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \\approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\sigma$ & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\Delta\\theta$\\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\\Delta c_n$\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.0 & 0.128 $\\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\\\\n0.1 & 0.130 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\\\\n0.2 & 0.135 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\\\\n0.5 & 0.175 $\\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\\% when exposure noise $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}\n\\label{tab:error_prop}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial ($+$36.9\\% and $+$57.9\\%). Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\\sigma = 0.5$ ($\\tau > 0.88$).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} (Appendix) reports ablation results on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). The dominant effect is joint versus staged estimation: staged estimation degrades $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ ($\\tau$ drops 8.3pp, $p < 0.001$), directly validating the OVB analysis; continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% over binary gating ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ does not affect rankings ($\\tau$ identical, $p = 0.34$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\\%, $p < 0.001$), confirming its role as a diagnostic component.\n",
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on LLM-as-a-judge protocols, where one or more LLM judges score model responses on structured benchmarks \citep{zheng2023mtbench}. Two well-documented sources of distortion affect these evaluations: \emph{judge bias}, where different judges assign systematically different scores to the same response, and \emph{benchmark contamination}, where models achieve inflated scores on items encountered during training. Both problems have received sustained research attention, producing separate toolkits for bias calibration and contamination detection. These toolkits treat the two distortions as independent phenomena, a separation that reflects their origins in different research communities rather than any principled argument for independence. Accurate ability ranking is essential for model selection, safety evaluation, and benchmark design; systematic distortion in estimated abilities undermines all three. Yet a practitioner who needs both corrections must apply them sequentially: first correct one source of distortion, then the other. This sequential approach produces unreliable ability estimates. In synthetic experiments with 50 models, 100 items, and 8 judges, sequential estimation yields ability root mean squared error (RMSE) of $0.254 \pm 0.045$, while joint estimation achieves $0.124 \pm 0.016$, a 51.2\% reduction (Wilcoxon $p < 10^{-6}$). The gap does not arise from estimation noise or algorithmic limitations of individual methods; it reflects structural confounding between judge bias and contamination that no sequential pipeline can resolve.
5	
6	Existing methods address these distortions in isolation. Judge bias corrections---CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, UDA \citep{uda2026}---remove systematic inter-judge differences without modeling contamination. Contamination detection tools---ConStat \citep{constat2024}, DCR \citep{dcr2025}, LogProber \citep{logprober2024}---adjust for memorization without accounting for judge effects (see Section~\ref{sec:related_work} for detailed review). A bias-first pipeline absorbs contamination-induced score inflation into ability estimates, because the bias model attributes all residual systematic variation to genuine ability. A contamination-first pipeline misattributes judge severity differences as contamination, because score elevation from a lenient judge is indistinguishable from elevation due to memorization without a joint model. In either ordering, the first correction stage produces estimates distorted by the unmodeled facet, and the second stage inherits this distortion.
7	
8	We formalize this confounding as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem \citep{angrist2009mostly, chernozhukov2022ovb}. Within the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) framework \citep{linacre1989mfrm}, the complete data-generating process includes both judge severity $d_j$ and a contamination interaction $c_n \cdot e_i$. Fitting a specification that omits the contamination term produces biased parameter estimates: the omission of $c_n \cdot e_i$ induces a specification bias that persists regardless of confounding structure, with an additional directional component when judge severity and contamination are non-orthogonal. The OVB framework predicts that the bias magnitude scales with the contamination susceptibility $c_n$ of each model, so the most contaminated models receive the largest upward distortion in estimated ability. For practical benchmark evaluations, this means that the more a model's training data overlaps with the benchmark, the more its ability is overestimated, precisely the failure mode that contamination correction is designed to prevent. Synthetic validation confirms the predicted pattern: the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), which shows that contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).
9	
10	We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which combines the multi-facet structure of MFRM \citep{linacre1989mfrm, jiao2025mfrm_llm} with the gating mechanism of Doubly-Gated Item Response Theory (DG-IRT) \citep{shu2013dgirt} to jointly estimate model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility in a single measurement framework. G-MFRM extends the MFRM linear predictor with a multiplicative contamination gating term $c_n \cdot e_i$, where $c_n \geq 0$ captures model-specific contamination susceptibility and $e_i \in [0,1]$ quantifies per-item exposure. When $c_n = 0$ or $e_i = 0$, the gating term vanishes and the model reduces to standard MFRM, so uncontaminated model-item pairs incur no distortion. Because the bilinear product $c_n \cdot e_i$ cannot be uniquely decomposed from response data alone, G-MFRM employs a three-stage calibration procedure: (1) standard MFRM warm-start to obtain initial parameter estimates, (2) external per-item exposure estimation from evidence independent of the response data, and (3) joint Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling with $e_i$ fixed from Stage~2. We provide a formal identifiability analysis (Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new}--\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}) that proves fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs (Section~\ref{sec:method}). A complementary error propagation analysis bounds the impact of Stage~2 measurement noise: when exposure estimation error satisfies $\sigma \leq 0.2$, ability RMSE degradation remains below 6\%. Figure~\ref{fig:framework} provides an overview of the complete framework.
11	
12	Our contributions are as follows:
13	\begin{enumerate}
14	    \item We identify and characterize, under a linear approximation to the logit link, the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination in LLM evaluation as an omitted variable bias problem, and show that sequential correction produces directional bias proportional to model contamination levels (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).
15	    \item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new}--\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\ref{sec:method}, Table~\ref{tab:error_prop}).
16	    \item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 10^{-6}$), with Kendall rank correlation $\tau = 0.922$ versus $\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
17	    \item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
18	\end{enumerate}
```

For practical benchmark evaluations, this means that the more a model's training data overlaps with the benchmark, the more its ability is overestimated, precisely the failure mode that contamination correction is designed to prevent. Synthetic validation confirms the predicted pattern: the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), which shows that contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\nWe propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which combines the multi-facet structure of MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, jiao2025mfrm_llm} with the gating mechanism of Doubly-Gated Item Response Theory (DG-IRT) \\citep{shu2013dgirt} to jointly estimate model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility in a single measurement framework. G-MFRM extends the MFRM linear predictor with a multiplicative contamination gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ captures model-specific contamination susceptibility and $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies per-item exposure. When $c_n = 0$ or $e_i = 0$, the gating term vanishes and the model reduces to standard MFRM, so uncontaminated model-item pairs incur no distortion. Because the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ cannot be uniquely decomposed from response data alone, G-MFRM employs a three-stage calibration procedure: (1) standard MFRM warm-start to obtain initial parameter estimates, (2) external per-item exposure estimation from evidence independent of the response data, and (3) joint Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling with $e_i$ fixed from Stage~2. We provide a formal identifiability analysis (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) that proves fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). A complementary error propagation analysis bounds the impact of Stage~2 measurement noise: when exposure estimation error satisfies $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, ability RMSE degradation remains below 6\\%. Figure~\\ref{fig:framework} provides an overview of the complete framework.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We identify and characterize, under a linear approximation to the logit link, the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination in LLM evaluation as an omitted variable bias problem, and show that sequential correction produces directional bias proportional to model contamination levels (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 10^{-6}$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
```json
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Introduction}",
          " \\label{sec:introduction}",
          " ",
          "-Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on LLM-as-a-judge protocols, where one or more LLM judges score model responses on structured benchmarks \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}. Two well-documented sources of distortion affect these evaluations: \\emph{judge bias}, where different judges assign systematically different scores to the same response, and \\emph{benchmark contamination}, where models achieve inflated scores on items encountered during training. Both problems have received sustained research attention, producing separate toolkits for bias calibration and contamination detection. These toolkits treat the two distortions as independent phenomena, a separation that reflects their origins in different research communities rather than any principled argument for independence. Accurate ability ranking is essential for model selection, safety evaluation, and benchmark design; systematic distortion in estimated abilities undermines all three. Yet a practitioner who needs both corrections must apply them sequentially: first correct one source of distortion, then the other. This sequential approach produces unreliable ability estimates. In synthetic experiments with 50 models, 100 items, and 8 judges, sequential estimation yields ability root mean squared error (RMSE) of $0.254 \\pm 0.045$, while joint estimation achieves $0.124 \\pm 0.016$, a 51.2\\% reduction (Wilcoxon $p < 10^{-6}$). The gap does not arise from estimation noise or algorithmic limitations of individual methods; it reflects structural confounding between judge bias and contamination that no sequential pipeline can resolve.",
          "+Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on LLM-as-a-judge protocols, where one or more LLM judges score model responses on structured benchmarks \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}. Two well-documented sources of distortion affect these evaluations: \\emph{judge bias}, where different judges assign systematically different scores to the same response, and \\emph{benchmark contamination}, where models achieve inflated scores on items encountered during training. Both problems have received sustained research attention, producing separate toolkits for bias calibration and contamination detection. These toolkits treat the two distortions as independent phenomena, a separation that reflects their origins in different research communities rather than any principled argument for independence. Accurate ability ranking is essential for model selection, safety evaluation, and benchmark design; systematic distortion in estimated abilities undermines all three. Yet a practitioner who needs both corrections must apply them sequentially: first correct one source of distortion, then the other. This sequential approach produces unreliable ability estimates. In synthetic experiments with 50 models, 100 items, and 8 judges, sequential estimation yields ability root mean squared error (RMSE) of $0.254 \\pm 0.045$, while joint estimation achieves $0.124 \\pm 0.016$, a 51.2\\% reduction (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). The gap does not arise from estimation noise or algorithmic limitations of individual methods; it reflects structural confounding between judge bias and contamination that no sequential pipeline can resolve.",
          " ",
          " Existing methods address these distortions in isolation. Judge bias corrections---CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, UDA \\citep{uda2026}---remove systematic inter-judge differences without modeling contamination. Contamination detection tools---ConStat \\citep{constat2024}, DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, LogProber \\citep{logprober2024}---adjust for memorization without accounting for judge effects (see Section~\\ref{sec:related_work} for detailed review). A bias-first pipeline absorbs contamination-induced score inflation into ability estimates, because the bias model attributes all residual systematic variation to genuine ability. A contamination-first pipeline misattributes judge severity differences as contamination, because score elevation from a lenient judge is indistinguishable from elevation due to memorization without a joint model. In either ordering, the first correction stage produces estimates distorted by the unmodeled facet, and the second stage inherits this distortion.",
          " "
```

- G-MFRM 在所有 judge count 下均优于 raw average 和 sequential baselines

- 效果随 judge 增加而增强——更多 judges 提供更好的 d_j/c_n 估计

### Tool result

_source line 3103 · 2026-05-23T03:19:03.977Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KNWD1YtgKRqmJUbm3tzQCi`</summary>

```text
42	All seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\tau = 0.786$, $\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\tau$ takes only ${\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$, $\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\Delta\tau$ 95\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\Delta\tau \geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\tau = 0.922$ vs.\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\theta_{\text{mini}} > \theta_{\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$, $\theta$-difference 95\% CI $[-0.227, +0.722]$).
43	
44	While the bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides decisive evidence that the contamination gating term captures genuine data structure: G-MFRM achieves $\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$, far exceeding the threshold of 2.0 for decisive model preference; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. The contamination parameters improve out-of-sample prediction, not merely in-sample fit.
45	
46	The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\hat{\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\bar{c} \approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\hat{\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix).
47	
48	\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}
49	\label{sec:error_prop_results}
50	
51	Section~\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\sigma$ remains below $\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).
```

```text
207	To assess sensitivity, we re-fit G-MFRM excluding all 99 self-judging observations (Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B judging their own responses). The resulting ranking yields $\tau = 0.857$, identical to the full-data G-MFRM point estimate, with a swap at ranks 2--3. Contamination susceptibility estimates $c_n$ shift by less than 0.15 for all models. This confirms that $d_j$ absorbs the bulk of self-evaluation bias and that rankings are robust to excluding self-judging pairs.
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
```

```json
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
    "oldString": "Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix).",
    "newString": "Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for full coverage across all judges and target models, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (5{,}661 observations; 44.7\\% fill rate of the full $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Eight target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score all model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro NUTS with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ and bulk/tail ESS \\citep{vehtari2021rhat}.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nRaw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nMFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nSequential & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nDCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nPref.\\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\n\\midrule\nG-MFRM (ours) & \\textbf{0.857} & \\textbf{0.952} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (8 target models, 9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\\tau$ takes only ${\\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$, $\\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\\Delta\\tau \\geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\\theta_{\\text{mini}} > \\theta_{\\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$, $\\theta$-difference 95\\% CI $[-0.227, +0.722]$).\n\nWhile the bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides decisive evidence that the contamination gating term captures genuine data structure: G-MFRM achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$, far exceeding the threshold of 2.0 for decisive model preference; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. The contamination parameters improve out-of-sample prediction, not merely in-sample fit.\n\nThe MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\\sigma$ remains below $\\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \\approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\sigma$ & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\Delta\\theta$\\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\\Delta c_n$\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.0 & 0.128 $\\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\\\\n0.1 & 0.130 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\\\\n0.2 & 0.135 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\\\\n0.5 & 0.175 $\\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\\% when exposure noise $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}\n\\label{tab:error_prop}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial ($+$36.9\\% and $+$57.9\\%). Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\\sigma = 0.5$ ($\\tau > 0.88$).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} (Appendix) reports ablation results on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). The dominant effect is joint versus staged estimation: staged estimation degrades $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ ($\\tau$ drops 8.3pp, $p < 0.001$), directly validating the OVB analysis; continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% over binary gating ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ does not affect rankings ($\\tau$ identical, $p = 0.34$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\\%, $p < 0.001$), confirming its role as a diagnostic component.\n",

Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",
          " ",
          " \\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}",
          " \\label{sec:error_prop_results}"
        ]
      }
    ],

    "replaceAll": false
  },
```

**2. (warning) Judge LOOCV Robustness 讨论**
Reviewer 发现移除 3 个特定 judge 后 G-MFRM τ 降至 baseline (0.786)：
- claude-opus-4.7 (d_j=+0.60, 最严厉)
- gpt-5.5 (d_j=+0.53, 次严厉)  
- llama-3.3-70b (d_j=-0.42, 次宽松)
移除其他 6 个 judge → τ 保持 0.857。

在 Judge Count Sensitivity appendix section 末尾加 1 段 LOOCV 讨论：
"Leave-one-judge-out analysis reveals that three judges—Claude Opus 4.7 (d_j = +0.60), GPT-5.5 (+0.53), and LLaMA-3.3-70B (−0.42)—are individually necessary for the ranking improvement. These judges span the severity spectrum, suggesting that contamination×judge interaction identification requires sufficient severity diversity. This finding provides practical guidance: judge panels should include models with diverse scoring tendencies rather than homogeneous judges."

编译确认 8 页正文。

```text
238	\section{Judge Count Sensitivity}
239	\label{app:judge_count}
240	
241	Table~\ref{tab:judge_count} reports ranking accuracy as a function of the number of judges $k$, obtained by subsampling from the full 9-judge panel (100 random subsets per $k$; $k = 9$ uses the full panel). G-MFRM outperforms both raw averaging and sequential estimation at all judge counts. Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\tau = 0.771 \to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. At $k \geq 4$, G-MFRM exceeds the full-panel baseline $\tau = 0.786$, suggesting that four diverse judges suffice for practical deployment.
242	
243	\begin{table}[h]
244	\centering
245	\small
246	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
247	\toprule
248	$k$ & Raw Avg & Sequential & G-MFRM & $\Delta\tau$ \\
249	\midrule
250	2 & $.755 \pm .088$ & $.764 \pm .096$ & $.771 \pm .081$ & $+.017$ \\
251	4 & $.779 \pm .041$ & $.786 \pm .048$ & $.800 \pm .045$ & $+.021$ \\
252	6 & $.800 \pm .030$ & $.807 \pm .048$ & $.821 \pm .051$ & $+.021$ \\
253	9 & $.786$ & $.786$ & $.857$ & $+.071$ \\
254	\bottomrule
255	\end{tabular}
256	\caption{Kendall $\tau$ with Chatbot Arena rankings as a function of judge count $k$. $\Delta\tau$ = G-MFRM $-$ Raw Average. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\pm$ std over 100 random judge subsets.}
257	\label{tab:judge_count}
258	\end{table}
259	
260	
261	\section{Per-Model Estimates}
262	\label{app:raw_scores}
263	
264	Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores} reports G-MFRM posterior estimates for all eight target models. All seven baselines produce identical rankings (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), with ability scores differing by $< 0.03$ logits across methods; G-MFRM is the only method that alters the ranking, swapping gpt-4o-mini and Qwen2.5-72B at ranks 2--3.
265	
266	\begin{table}[h]
267	\centering
```

```json
          " \\section{Judge Count Sensitivity}",
          " \\label{app:judge_count}",
          " ",
          "-Table~\\ref{tab:judge_count} reports ranking accuracy as a function of the number of judges $k$, obtained by subsampling from the full 9-judge panel (100 random subsets per $k$; $k = 9$ uses the full panel). G-MFRM outperforms both raw averaging and sequential estimation at all judge counts. Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\\tau = 0.771 \\to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. At $k \\geq 4$, G-MFRM exceeds the full-panel baseline $\\tau = 0.786$, suggesting that four diverse judges suffice for practical deployment.",
```

```json
  "old_string": "\\caption{Kendall $\\tau$ with Chatbot Arena rankings as a function of judge count $k$. $\\Delta\\tau$ = G-MFRM $-$ Raw Average. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\\pm$ std over 100 random judge subsets.}\n\\label{tab:judge_count}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Per-Model Estimates}",
  "new_string": "\\caption{Kendall $\\tau$ with Chatbot Arena rankings as a function of judge count $k$. $\\Delta\\tau$ = G-MFRM $-$ Raw Average. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\\pm$ std over 10 random judge subsets.}\n\\label{tab:judge_count}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-judge-out analysis reveals that three judges---Claude Opus 4.7 ($d_j = +0.60$), GPT-5.5 ($+0.53$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($-0.42$)---are individually necessary for the ranking improvement: removing any one of these reduces G-MFRM $\\tau$ to the baseline ($0.786$), while removing any of the other six judges preserves $\\tau = 0.857$. These three judges span the severity spectrum (the two strictest and the second-most lenient), suggesting that contamination$\\times$judge interaction identification requires sufficient severity diversity. This provides practical guidance: judge panels should include models with diverse scoring tendencies rather than homogeneous judges.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Model Estimates}"
}
```

```text
238	\section{Judge Count Sensitivity}
239	\label{app:judge_count}
240	
241	Table~\ref{tab:judge_count} reports ranking accuracy as a function of the number of judges $k$, obtained by subsampling from the full 9-judge panel (10 random subsets per $k$; $k = 9$ uses the full panel). G-MFRM outperforms both raw averaging and sequential estimation at all judge counts. Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\tau = 0.771 \to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. At $k \geq 4$, G-MFRM exceeds the full-panel baseline $\tau = 0.786$, suggesting that four diverse judges suffice for practical deployment.
242	
243	\begin{table}[h]
244	\centering
245	\small
246	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
247	\toprule
248	$k$ & Raw Avg & Sequential & G-MFRM & $\Delta\tau$ \\
249	\midrule
250	2 & $.755 \pm .088$ & $.764 \pm .096$ & $.771 \pm .081$ & $+.017$ \\
251	4 & $.779 \pm .041$ & $.786 \pm .048$ & $.800 \pm .045$ & $+.021$ \\
252	6 & $.800 \pm .030$ & $.807 \pm .048$ & $.821 \pm .051$ & $+.021$ \\
253	9 & $.786$ & $.786$ & $.857$ & $+.071$ \\
254	\bottomrule
255	\end{tabular}
256	\caption{Kendall $\tau$ with Chatbot Arena rankings as a function of judge count $k$. $\Delta\tau$ = G-MFRM $-$ Raw Average. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\pm$ std over 10 random judge subsets.}
257	\label{tab:judge_count}
258	\end{table}
259	
260	Leave-one-judge-out analysis reveals that three judges---Claude Opus 4.7 ($d_j = +0.60$), GPT-5.5 ($+0.53$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($-0.42$)---are individually necessary for the ranking improvement: removing any one of these reduces G-MFRM $\tau$ to the baseline ($0.786$), while removing any of the other six judges preserves $\tau = 0.857$. These three judges span the severity spectrum (the two strictest and the second-most lenient), suggesting that contamination$\times$judge interaction identification requires sufficient severity diversity. This provides practical guidance: judge panels should include models with diverse scoring tendencies rather than homogeneous judges.
261	
262	
263	\section{Per-Model Estimates}
264	\label{app:raw_scores}
265	
266	Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores} reports G-MFRM posterior estimates for all eight target models. All seven baselines produce identical rankings (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), with ability scores differing by $< 0.03$ logits across methods; G-MFRM is the only method that alters the ranking, swapping gpt-4o-mini and Qwen2.5-72B at ranks 2--3.
267	
268	\begin{table}[h]
269	\centering
270	\scriptsize
271	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
272	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
```

Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\\tau = 0.771 \\to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\\pm$ std over 10 random judge subsets.}\n\\label{tab:judge_count}\n\\end{table}\n\nLeave-one-judge-out analysis reveals that three judges---Claude Opus 4.7 ($d_j = +0.60$), GPT-5.5 ($+0.53$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($-0.42$)---are individually necessary for the ranking improvement: removing any one of these reduces G-MFRM $\\tau$ to the baseline ($0.786$), while removing any of the other six judges preserves $\\tau = 0.857$. These three judges span the severity spectrum (the two strictest and the second-most lenient), suggesting that contamination$\\times$judge interaction identification requires sufficient severity diversity. This provides practical guidance: judge panels should include models with diverse scoring tendencies rather than homogeneous judges.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Model Estimates}\n\\label{app:raw_scores}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:raw_scores} reports G-MFRM posterior estimates for all eight target models. All seven baselines produce identical rankings (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), with ability scores differing by $< 0.03$ logits across methods; G-MFRM is the only method that alters the ranking, swapping gpt-4o-mini and Qwen2.5-72B at ranks 2--3.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}",
```json
      "numLines": 35,
      "startLine": 238,
      "totalLines": 289
    }
```

```json
  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] Round 3 审稿修复 — 13 项批量 Patch，DDL 2026-05-26\n\n**投稿目标**: EMNLP 2026 ARR May cycle, 8 页正文限制\n\n**优先级排序**:\n\n## P0（格式/措辞 critical — 必须先做）\n\n1. **R3-N6 页面溢出**: Conclusion 溢出到第 9 页。压缩正文使其完全在 8 页内。优先压缩 Related Work（§5）或 §4.2 讨论段落。Conclusion 本身已经很短（4 句），不要再压缩 Conclusion。Limitations 不计入页数限制。\n\n2. **R3-N5 LOO 措辞过强**: §4.2 中将 LOO ΔELPD=-60 称为 \"decisive evidence\" — 但 ΔELPD 衡量的是 model fit，不是 ranking accuracy。改为 \"strong evidence for improved model fit\"，并显式说明 \"ΔELPD measures predictive fit, not ranking accuracy; ranking improvement (Δτ=+0.071) remains non-significant at N=8\"。\n\n3. **R1-W5 CI 下界标注**: 在 §4.2 主结果段落（不仅仅是 Limitations）中，所有涉及 credible interval 的地方加 qualifier：\"reported CIs should be interpreted as approximate lower bounds on true uncertainty (empirical coverage ~71%; see Limitations)\"。\n\n## P1（实质性修复）\n\n4. **R2-W3 §3.1 新颖性重写**: 重写 §3.1 末段。不要列 feature list（\"G-MFRM differs from DG-IRT in three ways...\"），而是强调方法论贡献：(a) 可辨识性分析（Propositions 1-2, Theorem 1）为 gated IRT 框架首次提供形式化条件，(b) Murphy-Topel error propagation 框架量化了 staged estimation 的不确定性传播——这两项是 DG-IRT 完全没有的。\n\n5. **R1-W10 C1 重新定位**: 将 Contribution 1 从独立 contribution 改为 motivating observation。具体措辞建议：\"We begin by formalizing the confounding between judge severity and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias within the MFRM framework (§2), motivating the joint calibration approach.\"\n\n6. **R1-W8 MCMC 引用修正**: 当前使用 Vehtari 2021 rank-normalized split-R̂ 方法但采用 Gelman 2013 的 R̂<1.05 阈值。修正为：引用 Gelman 2013 作为阈值来源，注明 \"we compute rank-normalized split-R̂ following Vehtari et al. [Director 指令] [Patch] Round 3 审稿修复 — 13 项批量 Patch，DDL 2026-05-26
```

**投稿目标**: EMNLP 2026 ARR May cycle, 8 页正文限制

**优先级排序**:

## P0（格式/措辞 critical — 必须先做）

1. **R3-N6 页面溢出**: Conclusion 溢出到第 9 页。压缩正文使其完全在 8 页内。优先压缩 Related Work（§5）或 §4.2 讨论段落。Conclusion 本身已经很短（4 句），不要再压缩 Conclusion。Limitations 不计入页数限制。

2. **R3-N5 LOO 措辞过强**: §4.2 中将 LOO ΔELPD=-60 称为 "decisive evidence" — 但 ΔELPD 衡量的是 model fit，不是 ranking accuracy。改为 "strong evidence for improved model fit"，并显式说明 "ΔELPD measures predictive fit, not ranking accuracy; ranking improvement (Δτ=+0.071) remains non-significant at N=8"。

3. **R1-W5 CI 下界标注**: 在 §4.2 主结果段落（不仅仅是 Limitations）中，所有涉及 credible interval 的地方加 qualifier："reported CIs should be interpreted as approximate lower bounds on true uncertainty (empirical coverage ~71%; see Limitations)"。

## P1（实质性修复）

4. **R2-W3 §3.1 新颖性重写**: 重写 §3.1 末段。不要列 feature list（"G-MFRM differs from DG-IRT in three ways..."），而是强调方法论贡献：(a) 可辨识性分析（Propositions 1-2, Theorem 1）为 gated IRT 框架首次提供形式化条件，(b) Murphy-Topel error propagation 框架量化了 staged estimation 的不确定性传播——这两项是 DG-IRT 完全没有的。

5. **R1-W10 C1 重新定位**: 将 Contribution 1 从独立 contribution 改为 motivating observation。具体措辞建议："We begin by formalizing the confounding between judge severity and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias within the MFRM framework (§2), motivating the joint calibration approach."

6. **R1-W8 MCMC 引用修正**: 当前使用 Vehtari 2021 rank-normalized split-R̂ 方法但采用 Gelman 2013 的 R̂<1.05 阈值。修正为：引用 Gelman 2013 作为阈值来源，注明 "we compute rank-normalized split-R̂ following Vehtari et al. (2021) but adopt the conventional R̂<1.05 threshold (Gelman et al., 2013)"。

7. **R1-W6 权重 sensitivity 表**: 在 Appendix 新增 "Stage 2 Weight Sensitivity" section（如果之前已有则确认数据完整）。表格内容：4 种权重配置（50/50, 75/25, 90/10, 100/0）的 downstream τ 和 exposure Spearman ρ。数据：所有 4 配置 τ 完全相同（0.786），ρ≥0.998。

8. **R3-N3 Power analysis Appendix**: 新增 Appendix section "Post-hoc Power Analysis"。内容：(a) effect size assumption: Δτ=0.071 from observed data, (b) 使用 permutation-based power function, (c) N=8 → ~50% power, N=17 → 80% power, N=25 → 95% power, (d) 结论：N=8 的 non-significant result 不能解读为"无效果"，仅为 underpowered。

## P2（Minor 修复）

9. **R3-N1 OVB 近似失效条件**: 在 Observation 1 后或 §2 末尾加 1-2 句："The linear approximation holds when c_n·e_i is small relative to the logit scale; for large contamination effects, the gated model (Eq. 4) captures the full nonlinear interaction without relying on this approximation."

10. **R3-N2 σ_crit 假设讨论**: 在 §3.3 或 error propagation 讨论中加 1 句："The Gaussian noise assumption in Eq. 6 is a first-order approximation; Table 2 provides empirical validation that θ̂ estimates remain robust under the heteroskedastic noise structure of real exposure estimates."

11. **R3-N4 Rank-1 limitation**: 在 Limitations section 增加一点（调整现有 "five limitations" 为 "six limitations"）："Sixth, the bilinear c_n·e_i term assumes rank-1 contamination interaction; relaxing this to low-rank decomposition would capture heterogeneous contamination patterns but increases parameters and may compromise the identifiability conditions of Theorem 1."

12. **R3-MR + R1-W13 缺失引用**: 添加 3 篇 references 到 bib 并在 Related Work 适当位置引用：
    - Polo et al. 2024 "tinyBenchmarks: evaluating LLMs with fewer examples"
    - Ye et al. 2024 "Justice or Prejudice? Quantifying Biases in LLM-as-a-Judge"
    - Panickssery et al. 2024 "LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations"

13. **R1-W3 Theorem 1 证明扩展**: 在 Appendix 的 Theorem 1 证明中：(a) 补充 Cauchy-Schwarz 应用条件的详细推导（不仅是引用结论），(b) 加 1 段讨论 local vs global identifiability："Theorem 1 establishes local identifiability at generic parameter values; global identifiability follows from the connected design condition ensuring a unique solution up to location normalization."

**引用验证**: 这次不用验证已有引用。仅确保新增 3 篇引用的 bib 条目格式正确。

**编译**: 所有修改完成后编译一次，确保 0 error、8 页正文、引用无 undefined。

```text
12	Our contributions are as follows:
13	\begin{enumerate}
14	    \item We identify and characterize, under a linear approximation to the logit link, the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination in LLM evaluation as an omitted variable bias problem, and show that sequential correction produces directional bias proportional to model contamination levels (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).
15	    \item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new}--\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\ref{sec:method}, Table~\ref{tab:error_prop}).
16	    \item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\tau = 0.922$ versus $\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
17	    \item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
18	\end{enumerate}
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}
5	CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias through round-robin judge assignment but treats residual score variation as genuine ability. UDA \citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor adjustment reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\%, though the correction is a black box with no contamination mechanism. \citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability through $J/\Delta J$ diagnostics, identifying sign-reversal failure modes. All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Contamination Detection and Adjustment.}
8	ConStat \citep{constat2024} estimates contamination via reference model comparison ($r^2 = 0.94$), establishing the framework G-MFRM's Stage~2 builds upon. DCR \citep{dcr2025} adjusts scores through fuzzy inference rules; LogProber \citep{logprober2024} detects memorization from token-level log-probabilities; Min-K\% \citep{mink2024} uses perplexity signals. All assume a fixed rater, so judge severity can masquerade as contamination. \citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side contamination (same-family leakage $\approx$ 8.9\%), complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus.
9	
10	\paragraph{Contamination Avoidance.}
11	LiveBench \citep{livebench2025} and TRUCE \citep{truce2024} prevent contamination by design through frequently updated items and confidential computing, respectively. These are complementary: avoidance reduces exposed items, while G-MFRM corrects for residual contamination.
12	
13	\paragraph{IRT and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}
14	\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to LLM raters without contamination terms. \citet{uto2020generalized} extend MFRM with Bayesian HMC estimation. \citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose judge reliability via Graded Response Models, and PSN-IRT \citep{zhou2025psnirt} extends IRT to neural architectures---none include contamination facets. The closest antecedent, DG-IRT \citep{shu2013dgirt}, is compared in Section~\ref{sec:model_spec}. G-MFRM bridges these threads by jointly modeling judge bias and contamination (\S\ref{sec:confounding}).
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for full coverage across all judges and target models, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (5{,}661 observations; 44.7\% fill rate of the full $N \times I \times J$ tensor). Eight target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \citep{zheng2023mtbench}.
8	
9	\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score all model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}).
10	
11	\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\S\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).
12	
13	\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro NUTS with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ and bulk/tail ESS \citep{vehtari2021rhat}.
14	
15	\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}
16	\label{sec:main_results}
17	
18	We compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\tau$ and Spearman $\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\small
23	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
24	\toprule
25	\textbf{Method} & $\tau$ & $\rho$ \\
26	\midrule
27	Raw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
28	MFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
29	Sequential & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
30	CyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
31	CyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
32	DCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
33	Pref.\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
34	\midrule
35	G-MFRM (ours) & \textbf{0.857} & \textbf{0.952} \\
36	\bottomrule
37	\end{tabular}
38	\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (8 target models, 9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero).}
39	\label{tab:main_results}
40	\end{table}
41	
42	All seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\tau = 0.786$, $\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\tau$ takes only ${\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$, $\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\Delta\tau$ 95\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\Delta\tau \geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\tau = 0.922$ vs.\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\theta_{\text{mini}} > \theta_{\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$, $\theta$-difference 95\% CI $[-0.227, +0.722]$).
43	
44	While the bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides decisive evidence that the contamination gating term captures genuine data structure: G-MFRM achieves $\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$, far exceeding the threshold of 2.0 for decisive model preference; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. The contamination parameters improve out-of-sample prediction, not merely in-sample fit.
45	
46	The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\hat{\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\bar{c} \approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\hat{\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \geq 2$, with $\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_count}).
47	
48	\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}
49	\label{sec:error_prop_results}
50	
51	Section~\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\sigma$ remains below $\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).
52	
53	\begin{table}[t]
54	\centering
55	\small
56	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
57	\toprule
58	$\sigma$ & $\theta$-RMSE & $\Delta\theta$\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\Delta c_n$\% \\
59	\midrule
60	0.0 & 0.128 $\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\
61	0.1 & 0.130 $\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\
62	0.2 & 0.135 $\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\
63	0.5 & 0.175 $\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\
64	\bottomrule
65	\end{tabular}
66	\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\% when exposure noise $\sigma \leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}
67	\label{tab:error_prop}
68	\end{table}
69	
70	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\%; beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial ($+$36.9\% and $+$57.9\%). Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\sigma = 0.5$ ($\tau > 0.88$).
71	
72	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} (Appendix) reports ablation results on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). The dominant effect is joint versus staged estimation: staged estimation degrades $\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\times$ ($\tau$ drops 8.3pp, $p < 0.001$), directly validating the OVB analysis; continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\% over binary gating ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ does not affect rankings ($\tau$ identical, $p = 0.34$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\%, $p < 0.001$), confirming its role as a diagnostic component.
73	
```
