      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for full coverage across all judges and target models, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (5{,}661 observations; 44.7\\% fill rate of the full $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Eight target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score all model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro NUTS with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ and bulk/tail ESS \\citep{vehtari2021rhat}.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nRaw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nMFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nSequential & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nDCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nPref.\\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\n\\midrule\nG-MFRM (ours) & \\textbf{0.857} & \\textbf{0.952} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (8 target models, 9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\\tau$ takes only ${\\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$, $\\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\\Delta\\tau \\geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\\theta_{\\text{mini}} > \\theta_{\\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$, $\\theta$-difference 95\\% CI $[-0.227, +0.722]$).\n\nWhile the bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides decisive evidence that the contamination gating term captures genuine data structure: G-MFRM achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$, far exceeding the threshold of 2.0 for decisive model preference; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. The contamination parameters improve out-of-sample prediction, not merely in-sample fit.\n\nThe MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\\sigma$ remains below $\\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \\approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\sigma$ & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\Delta\\theta$\\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\\Delta c_n$\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.0 & 0.128 $\\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\\\\n0.1 & 0.130 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\\\\n0.2 & 0.135 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\\\\n0.5 & 0.175 $\\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\\% when exposure noise $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}\n\\label{tab:error_prop}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial ($+$36.9\\% and $+$57.9\\%). Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\\sigma = 0.5$ ($\\tau > 0.88$).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} (Appendix) reports ablation results on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). The dominant effect is joint versus staged estimation: staged estimation degrades $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ ($\\tau$ drops 8.3pp, $p < 0.001$), directly validating the OVB analysis; continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% over binary gating ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ does not affect rankings ($\\tau$ identical, $p = 0.34$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\\%, $p < 0.001$), confirming its role as a diagnostic component.\n",

```text
13	\subsection{Model Specification}
14	\label{sec:model_spec}
15	
16	G-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}):
17	%
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:gmfrm}
20	\log \frac{P(Y_{nij} \geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i,
21	\end{equation}
22	%
23	where all terms carry the definitions from Section~\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \cdot e_i$ separates the model-specific and item-specific components of contamination: $c_n \geq 0$ captures how much model $n$ benefits from memorized benchmark content, while $e_i \in [0,1]$ quantifies how much of item $i$ has leaked into training corpora. When $c_n = 0$ (a clean model) or $e_i = 0$ (a novel item), the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM. This graceful degradation ensures that G-MFRM imposes no distortion on uncontaminated model-item pairs.
24	
25	We enforce non-negativity and sparsity of $c_n$ through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior. A global shrinkage scale $\sigma_c \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \cdot \sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. The remaining priors are $\theta_n \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and standard uniform on thresholds $\tau_k$.
26	
27	The closest antecedent is the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \citet{shu2013dgirt}, which introduced a binary gating mechanism to handle item preknowledge in educational testing. G-MFRM differs in three respects. First, exposure $e_i$ is continuous rather than binary, allowing partial contamination to be modeled. Second, the multi-facet structure incorporates judge severity $d_j$ as an explicit nuisance parameter, whereas DG-IRT assumes a single-rater design. Third, exposure is externally estimated rather than treated as known, necessitating the staged procedure and error propagation analysis that follow.
28	
29	\subsection{Staged Estimation}
30	\label{sec:staged}
31	
32	Joint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.
33	
34	\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\hat{\theta}, \hat{b}, \hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.
35	
36	\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data used in Stages~1 and~3. The primary signal is \emph{reference model anchoring}: using a fixed reference model (e.g., gpt-4-0314) as anchor, we compute win-rate residuals for each target model against difficulty-adjusted expectations; items where target models systematically outperform the anchor beyond what difficulty predicts are flagged as potentially exposed. The secondary signal is \emph{cross-model agreement}: items receiving uniformly high scores across architecturally diverse models suggest exposure rather than genuine easiness, quantified via $z$-score standardization of the inter-model agreement distribution. These signals are combined as a weighted composite (75\% anchoring, 25\% agreement), clipped to $[0, 1]$, and treated as fixed in Stage~3. Where model log-probabilities are accessible, LogProber can provide an additional gray-box memorization signal, though it is not used in our current experiments.
37	
38	\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via NUTS \citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \S\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as
39	%
40	\begin{align}
41	\label{eq:contam_prior}
42	\sigma_c &\sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \quad c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \nonumber \\
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01G7eLKZ5U5b4Lf2YdUXVScm`</summary>

```text
20	When a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\tilde{\theta}_n$, $\tilde{b}_i$, and $\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.
21	
22	The consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.
23	
24	\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]
25	\label{obs:ovb}
26	Let the full model be $\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\tilde{\eta}_{nijk} = \tilde{\theta}_n - \tilde{b}_i - \tilde{d}_j - \tau_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}
27	\begin{equation}
28	\label{eq:ovb}
29	\mathrm{plim}(\tilde{\theta}_n - \theta_n) = \gamma_n \cdot c_n,
30	\end{equation}
31	where $\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\, c_n \cdot e_i) \neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
32	\end{observation}
33	
34	The bias combines a specification component from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$, which persists regardless of confounding, with a confounding component from non-orthogonality between $d_j$ and $c_n \cdot e_i$. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\rho \in \{0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9\}$, indicating that the omission of $c_n \cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver (Figure~\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}).
35	
36	This confounding undermines both sequential estimation strategies. A bias-first approach, as in CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, calibrates $d_j$ before estimating ability, but the resulting $\hat{\theta}$ is already inflated for contaminated models because the omitted $c_n \cdot e_i$ term has been absorbed into the ability estimates. A contamination-first approach, as in DCR \citep{dcr2025}, adjusts for contamination before addressing judge effects, but judge severity is then misattributed as contamination signal. Synthetic validation confirms this failure: with $N = 50$ models, $I = 100$ items, $J = 8$ judges, and 20 random seeds, the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$). As illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:ovb_bias}, the bias is not random but directional: contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings.
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	\centering
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0193PVppRPPThzy1v7hqXhA4`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}}
2	\label{app:proof}
3	
4	We prove that the G-MFRM is locally identifiable under the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}. Following \citet{rothenberg1971}, a parametric model is locally identifiable at the true parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\psi}_0$ if and only if the Fisher information matrix $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_0)$ is non-singular.
5	
6	\paragraph{Setup.} The G-MFRM parameter vector is $\boldsymbol{\psi} = (\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c}, \mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$, where $\boldsymbol{\theta} \in \mathbb{R}^{N-1}$ (after the location constraint $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$), $\mathbf{c} \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^N$, $\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^I$, $\mathbf{d} \in \mathbb{R}^J$, and $\boldsymbol{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^{K-1}$. The exposure vector $\mathbf{e} = (e_1, \ldots, e_I)$ is fixed externally and treated as known. The linear predictor is $\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i$, with partial derivatives:
7	%
8	\begin{align*}
9	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial \theta_n} &= 1, \quad
10	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial c_n} = e_i, \quad
11	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial b_i} = -1, \\
12	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial d_j} &= -1, \quad
13	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial \tau_k} = -1.
14	\end{align*}
15	
16	\paragraph{Fisher information weights.} For each observation cell $(n,i,j)$, the Fisher information weight under the cumulative logit model is
17	%
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:fisher_weight}
20	w_{nij} = \sum_{k=1}^{K} \frac{\bigl[\sigma'(\eta_{nijk}) - \sigma'(\eta_{nij,k+1})\bigr]^2}{P(Y_{nij} = k)},
21	\end{equation}
22	%
23	where $\sigma(x) = (1 + e^{-x})^{-1}$, $\sigma'(x) = \sigma(x)(1 - \sigma(x))$, and we set $\sigma'(\eta_{nij,0}) = \sigma'(\eta_{nij,K+1}) = 0$ at the boundaries. Since $P(Y_{nij} = k) > 0$ for all categories and $\sigma'(\cdot) > 0$, we have $w_{nij} > 0$ for all $(n,i,j)$.
24	
25	\paragraph{Step 1: The $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix.} For each model $n$, the $2 \times 2$ Fisher information submatrix is
26	%
27	\begin{equation}
28	\label{eq:fisher_block}
29	\mathbf{F}_n = \sum_{i=1}^{I} \sum_{j=1}^{J} w_{nij}
30	\begin{pmatrix} 1 & e_i \\ e_i & e_i^2 \end{pmatrix}.
31	\end{equation}
32	%
33	Its determinant is
34	%
35	\begin{multline}
36	\det \mathbf{F}_n = \Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\Bigr)\Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\, e_i^2\Bigr) \\
37	- \Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\, e_i\Bigr)^2 = W_n^2 \cdot \mathrm{Var}_w(e_i),
38	\end{multline}
39	%
40	where $W_n = \sum_{i,j} w_{nij}$ and $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \overline{e^2}_n - \bar{e}_n^2$ is the weighted variance of exposure with weights $w_{nij}$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) \geq 0$, with equality if and only if $e_i$ is constant across all items with $w_{nij} > 0$. Under conditions~(i) and~(iii), the observed items for model $n$ include at least two distinct exposure values with $w_{nij} > 0$ for the corresponding cells. Therefore $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) > 0$, $\det \mathbf{F}_n > 0$, and each $\mathbf{F}_n$ is positive definite.
41	
42	\paragraph{Step 2: Item and judge blocks.} Under the connected design (condition~i), each item $i$ is observed by at least one $(n,j)$ pair, so the item difficulty block has positive diagonal entries $[\mathbf{F}_{bb}]_{ii} = \sum_{n,j} w_{nij} > 0$ (summing over observed cells). Similarly, each judge $j$ observes at least one $(n,i)$ pair, giving $[\mathbf{F}_{dd}]_{jj} = \sum_{n,i} w_{nij} > 0$. Condition~(ii) ($J \geq 2$) ensures the judge block has sufficient dimension to separate judge effects from the intercept. Both blocks are positive definite.
43	
44	\paragraph{Step 3: Schur complement argument.} The full Fisher information matrix $\mathbf{F}$ contains off-diagonal blocks coupling the parameter groups. Partition as
45	%
46	\begin{equation*}
47	\mathbf{F} = \begin{pmatrix} \mathbf{A} & \mathbf{B} \\ \mathbf{B}^\top & \mathbf{D} \end{pmatrix},
48	\end{equation*}
49	%
50	where $\mathbf{A} = \mathrm{blockdiag}(\mathbf{F}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{F}_N)$ collects all $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrices and $\mathbf{D}$ collects the $(\mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ blocks. From Step~1, $\mathbf{A}$ is positive definite. The Schur complement
51	%
52	\begin{equation}
53	\label{eq:schur}
54	\mathbf{S} = \mathbf{D} - \mathbf{B}^\top \mathbf{A}^{-1} \mathbf{B}
55	\end{equation}
56	%
57	equals the conditional Fisher information for $(\mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ given $(\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c})$. Conditioning on $(\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c})$ with fixed $\mathbf{e}$ reduces the model to a standard MFRM with known offsets $c_n \cdot e_i$, whose identifiability is established by \citet[Chapter~5]{sanmartin2016} under conditions~(i), (ii), and~(iv). Therefore $\mathbf{S}$ is positive definite.
58	
59	Since both $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{S}$ are positive definite:
60	%
61	\begin{equation*}
62	\det \mathbf{F} = \det \mathbf{A} \cdot \det \mathbf{S} > 0,
63	\end{equation*}
64	%
65	establishing non-singularity of $\mathbf{F}$ and hence local identifiability. \qed
66	
```

By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) \\geq 0$, with equality if and only if $e_i$ is constant across all items with $w_{nij} > 0$. Under conditions~(i) and~(iii), the observed items for model $n$ include at least two distinct exposure values with $w_{nij} > 0$ for the corresponding cells. Therefore $\\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) > 0$, $\\det \\mathbf{F}_n > 0$, and each $\\mathbf{F}_n$ is positive definite.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 2: Item and judge blocks.} Under the connected design (condition~i), each item $i$ is observed by at least one $(n,j)$ pair, so the item difficulty block has positive diagonal entries $[\\mathbf{F}_{bb}]_{ii} = \\sum_{n,j} w_{nij} > 0$ (summing over observed cells). Similarly, each judge $j$ observes at least one $(n,i)$ pair, giving $[\\mathbf{F}_{dd}]_{jj} = \\sum_{n,i} w_{nij} > 0$. Condition~(ii) ($J \\geq 2$) ensures the judge block has sufficient dimension to separate judge effects from the intercept. Both blocks are positive definite.\n\n\\paragraph{Step 3: Schur complement argument.} The full Fisher information matrix $\\mathbf{F}$ contains off-diagonal blocks coupling the parameter groups. Partition as\n%\n\\begin{equation*}\n\\mathbf{F} = \\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{A} & \\mathbf{B} \\\\ \\mathbf{B}^\\top & \\mathbf{D} \\end{pmatrix},\n\\end{equation*}\n%\nwhere $\\mathbf{A} = \\mathrm{blockdiag}(\\mathbf{F}_1, \\ldots, \\mathbf{F}_N)$ collects all $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrices and $\\mathbf{D}$ collects the $(\\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ blocks. From Step~1, $\\mathbf{A}$ is positive definite. The Schur complement\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:schur}\n\\mathbf{S} = \\mathbf{D} - \\mathbf{B}^\\top \\mathbf{A}^{-1} \\mathbf{B}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nequals the conditional Fisher information for $(\\mathbf{b}, \\mathbf{d}, \\boldsymbol{\\tau})$ given $(\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c})$. Conditioning on $(\\boldsymbol{\\theta}, \\mathbf{c})$ with fixed $\\mathbf{e}$ reduces the model to a standard MFRM with known offsets $c_n \\cdot e_i$, whose identifiability is established by \\citet[Chapter~5]{sanmartin2016} under conditions~(i), (ii), and~(iv). Therefore $\\mathbf{S}$ is positive definite.\n\nSince both $\\mathbf{A}$ and $\\mathbf{S}$ are positive definite:\n%\n\\begin{equation*}\n\\det \\mathbf{F} = \\det \\mathbf{A} \\cdot \\det \\mathbf{S} > 0,\n\\end{equation*}\n%\nestablishing non-singularity of $\\mathbf{F}$ and hence local identifiability. \\qed\n",
      "numLines": 66,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 297
    }

```text
85	\subsection{Error Propagation}
86	\label{sec:error_prop}
87	
88	The staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.
89	
90	Let $\hat{e}_i = e_i + \epsilon_i$ with $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is
91	%
92	\begin{equation}
93	\label{eq:attenuation}
94	\lambda(\sigma) = \frac{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \sigma^2},
95	\end{equation}
96	%
97	so attenuation reaches 50\% when $\sigma = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic DGP ($\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\sigma \approx 0.2$.\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. We quantify robustness to these violations empirically in Section~\ref{sec:experiments}.}
98	
99	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} shows the empirical degradation across noise levels (see also Figure~\ref{fig:error_prop} in the Appendix). Below the critical threshold ($\sigma \leq 0.2$), degradation is mild: $\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\% (from $0.128 \pm 0.018$ to $0.135 \pm 0.018$) and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\% (from $0.533 \pm 0.187$ to $0.575 \pm 0.198$). At $\sigma = 0.1$, the increases are only 1.4\% and 2.3\% respectively. Beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe: $\theta$-RMSE rises 36.9\% and $c_n$-RMSE rises 57.9\%. Contamination estimates $c_n$ are more sensitive than ability estimates $\theta_n$ because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$ (multiplicative coupling), while $\theta_n$ contributes additively and is anchored by all observations regardless of exposure.
100	
101	These bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability.
102	
103	\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}
104	\label{sec:fair_scores}
```

1	We identify five limitations. First, although Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\Delta\tau = 0.071$ at $\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \approx 17$ models for 80\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage is 71\% (50 Monte Carlo seeds), below the 95\% nominal level \citep{talts2018sbc}, indicating moderate posterior overconfidence likely due to the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint and NUTS tree depth saturation. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected, but reported CIs should be interpreted as lower bounds on true uncertainty. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted.
2	
3	Code and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL].
4

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (top-4 models' 95\\% CIs exclude zero, though posterior CI coverage is 71\\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation provides decisive model preference for G-MFRM over standard MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = -60$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$).\n\\end{abstract}",

  "old_string": "Our contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We identify and characterize, under a linear approximation to the logit link, the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination in LLM evaluation as an omitted variable bias problem, and show that sequential correction produces directional bias proportional to model contamination levels (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
  "new_string": "We begin by formalizing the confounding between judge severity and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias within the MFRM framework (\\S\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}), motivating the joint calibration approach. Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}"

    "oldString": "Our contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We identify and characterize, under a linear approximation to the logit link, the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination in LLM evaluation as an omitted variable bias problem, and show that sequential correction produces directional bias proportional to model contamination levels (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "newString": "We begin by formalizing the confounding between judge severity and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias within the MFRM framework (\\S\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}), motivating the joint calibration approach. Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge Language Model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on LLM-as-a-judge protocols, where one or more LLM judges score model responses on structured benchmarks \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}. Two well-documented sources of distortion affect these evaluations: \\emph{judge bias}, where different judges assign systematically different scores to the same response, and \\emph{benchmark contamination}, where models achieve inflated scores on items encountered during training. Both problems have received sustained research attention, producing separate toolkits for bias calibration and contamination detection. These toolkits treat the two distortions as independent phenomena, a separation that reflects their origins in different research communities rather than any principled argument for independence. Accurate ability ranking is essential for model selection, safety evaluation, and benchmark design; systematic distortion in estimated abilities undermines all three. Yet a practitioner who needs both corrections must apply them sequentially: first correct one source of distortion, then the other. This sequential approach produces unreliable ability estimates. In synthetic experiments with 50 models, 100 items, and 8 judges, sequential estimation yields ability root mean squared error (RMSE) of $0.254 \\pm 0.045$, while joint estimation achieves $0.124 \\pm 0.016$, a 51.2\\% reduction (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). The gap does not arise from estimation noise or algorithmic limitations of individual methods; it reflects structural confounding between judge bias and contamination that no sequential pipeline can resolve.\n\nExisting methods address these distortions in isolation. Judge bias corrections---CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, UDA \\citep{uda2026}---remove systematic inter-judge differences without modeling contamination. Contamination detection tools---ConStat \\citep{constat2024}, DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, LogProber \\citep{logprober2024}---adjust for memorization without accounting for judge effects (see Section~\\ref{sec:related_work} for detailed review). A bias-first pipeline absorbs contamination-induced score inflation into ability estimates, because the bias model attributes all residual systematic variation to genuine ability. A contamination-first pipeline misattributes judge severity differences as contamination, because score elevation from a lenient judge is indistinguishable from elevation due to memorization without a joint model. In either ordering, the first correction stage produces estimates distorted by the unmodeled facet, and the second stage inherits this distortion.\n\nWe formalize this confounding as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem \\citep{angrist2009mostly, chernozhukov2022ovb}. Within the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) framework \\citep{linacre1989mfrm}, the complete data-generating process includes both judge severity $d_j$ and a contamination interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Fitting a specification that omits the contamination term produces biased parameter estimates: the omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$ induces a specification bias that persists regardless of confounding structure, with an additional directional component when judge severity and contamination are non-orthogonal. The OVB framework predicts that the bias magnitude scales with the contamination susceptibility $c_n$ of each model, so the most contaminated models receive the largest upward distortion in estimated ability. For practical benchmark evaluations, this means that the more a model's training data overlaps with the benchmark, the more its ability is overestimated, precisely the failure mode that contamination correction is designed to prevent. Synthetic validation confirms the predicted pattern: the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), which shows that contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\nWe propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which combines the multi-facet structure of MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, jiao2025mfrm_llm} with the gating mechanism of Doubly-Gated Item Response Theory (DG-IRT) \\citep{shu2013dgirt} to jointly estimate model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility in a single measurement framework. G-MFRM extends the MFRM linear predictor with a multiplicative contamination gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ captures model-specific contamination susceptibility and $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies per-item exposure. When $c_n = 0$ or $e_i = 0$, the gating term vanishes and the model reduces to standard MFRM, so uncontaminated model-item pairs incur no distortion. Because the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ cannot be uniquely decomposed from response data alone, G-MFRM employs a three-stage calibration procedure: (1) standard MFRM warm-start to obtain initial parameter estimates, (2) external per-item exposure estimation from evidence independent of the response data, and (3) joint Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling with $e_i$ fixed from Stage~2. We provide a formal identifiability analysis (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) that proves fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). A complementary error propagation analysis bounds the impact of Stage~2 measurement noise: when exposure estimation error satisfies $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, ability RMSE degradation remains below 6\\%. Figure~\\ref{fig:framework} provides an overview of the complete framework.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We identify and characterize, under a linear approximation to the logit link, the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination in LLM evaluation as an omitted variable bias problem, and show that sequential correction produces directional bias proportional to model contamination levels (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Confounding in LLM Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:confounding}\n\nThe Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) extends classical Rasch measurement to settings with multiple rater facets \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm}. Originating in educational measurement, the model treats rater variation as a nuisance facet to be estimated and removed rather than a source of noise to be averaged over. In the LLM evaluation context, where $J$ judges score $N$ models across $I$ items, the MFRM specifies\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\tau_k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\tau_k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $Y_{nij}$ is the ordinal rating by judge $j$ for model $n$ on item $i$, $\\theta_n$ is the ability of model $n$ ($n = 1, \\ldots, N$), $b_i$ is the difficulty of item $i$ ($i = 1, \\ldots, I$), $d_j$ is the severity of judge $j$ ($j = 1, \\ldots, J$), and $\\tau_k$ is the threshold for category $k$. All facets reside on a common logit scale, so model ability is directly comparable after adjustment for item difficulty and judge severity. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} applied this framework to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, showing that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects in multi-judge panels.\n\nHowever, standard MFRM assumes that all systematic sources of score variation are captured by the modeled facets. This assumption breaks down when some models have been exposed to benchmark items during training \\citep{dcr2025}. Such contamination inflates scores on exposed items through memorization rather than genuine ability. We represent this effect as an interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ is the contamination susceptibility of model $n$ and $e_i \\in [0, 1]$ is the exposure level of item $i$. The complete data-generating process becomes\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:full}\n\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nWhen a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$, $\\tilde{b}_i$, and $\\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.\n\nThe consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.\n\n\\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]\n\\label{obs:ovb}\nLet the full model be $\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\\tilde{\\eta}_{nijk} = \\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\tilde{b}_i - \\tilde{d}_j - \\tau_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \\citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:ovb}\n\\mathrm{plim}(\\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\theta_n) = \\gamma_n \\cdot c_n,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\\, c_n \\cdot e_i) \\neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{observation}\n\nThe bias combines a specification component from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$, which persists regardless of confounding, with a confounding component from non-orthogonality between $d_j$ and $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\\rho \\in \\{0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9\\}$, indicating that the omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}).\n\nThis confounding undermines both sequential estimation strategies. A bias-first approach, as in CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, calibrates $d_j$ before estimating ability, but the resulting $\\hat{\\theta}$ is already inflated for contaminated models because the omitted $c_n \\cdot e_i$ term has been absorbed into the ability estimates. A contamination-first approach, as in DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, adjusts for contamination before addressing judge effects, but judge severity is then misattributed as contamination signal. Synthetic validation confirms this failure: with $N = 50$ models, $I = 100$ items, $J = 8$ judges, and 20 random seeds, the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$). As illustrated in Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}, the bias is not random but directional: contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_ovb_bias}\n\\caption{Sequential estimation bias ($\\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{seq}} - \\theta_{\\mathrm{true}}$) versus true contamination susceptibility $c_n$. Pearson $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$) confirms systematic overestimation of contaminated models. Synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds).}\n\\label{fig:ovb_bias}\n\\end{figure}",

A global shrinkage scale $\\sigma_c \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \\geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. 2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - G-MFRM: Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model with contamination gating term c_n·e_i
   - OVB: Omitted Variable Bias — sequential correction produces biased ability estimates
   - LOO-CV: ΔELPD_LOO = −60.0 (SE=9.4), |Δ/SE|=6.4 — measures predictive fit, NOT ranking accuracy
   - Bootstrap Δτ: 95% CI [−0.071, 0.071] includes zero → ranking improvement NOT significant at N=8
   - Connected design: 44.7% fill rate (5661/12672), NOT fully crossed
   - 9 judges: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, Qwen3-235B
   - 8 target models with θ and c_n posterior estimates from exp-003b
   - Self-evaluation: Qwen2.5-72B (win rate=1.000) and LLaMA-3.3-70B (win rate=0.912) as both judge and target
   - Judge Count Sensitivity: τ monotonically increases 0.771→0.857 with k=2→9; k≥4 exceeds baseline
   - 3 critical judges for ranking: Claude Opus, GPT-5.5, LLaMA-3.3-70B (span severity spectrum)
   - CI coverage: 71% (below 95% nominal) — CIs are lower bounds on true uncertainty
   - Power analysis: N≈17 for 80% power at Δτ=0.071; N=8 yields ~50%

        "content": "\\section{The G-MFRM Framework}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_framework}\n\\caption{Overview of the G-MFRM three-stage calibration procedure. Stage~1 fits a standard MFRM to obtain initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$. Stage~2 estimates per-item exposure $e_i$ from external evidence (reference model anchoring and cross-model agreement). Stage~3 performs joint MCMC inference with $e_i$ fixed, recovering contamination-corrected ability $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$, contamination susceptibility $c_n$, and judge severity $d_j$.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Model Specification}\n\\label{sec:model_spec}\n\nG-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}):\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gmfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere all terms carry the definitions from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ separates the model-specific and item-specific components of contamination: $c_n \\geq 0$ captures how much model $n$ benefits from memorized benchmark content, while $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies how much of item $i$ has leaked into training corpora. When $c_n = 0$ (a clean model) or $e_i = 0$ (a novel item), the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM. This graceful degradation ensures that G-MFRM imposes no distortion on uncontaminated model-item pairs.\n\nWe enforce non-negativity and sparsity of $c_n$ through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior. A global shrinkage scale $\\sigma_c \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \\geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. The remaining priors are $\\theta_n \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and standard uniform on thresholds $\\tau_k$.\n\nThe closest antecedent is the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt}, which introduced binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing. Beyond extending DG-IRT to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, G-MFRM's primary methodological contributions are analytical: the identifiability analysis (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) provides the first formal conditions under which gated IRT parameters are separable, and the Murphy--Topel error propagation framework (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}) quantifies how staged estimation uncertainty propagates to downstream parameter estimates---neither of which exists for DG-IRT or any prior gated measurement model.\n\n\\subsection{Staged Estimation}\n\\label{sec:staged}\n\nJoint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data used in Stages~1 and~3. The primary signal is \\emph{reference model anchoring}: using a fixed reference model (e.g., gpt-4-0314) as anchor, we compute win-rate residuals for each target model against difficulty-adjusted expectations; items where target models systematically outperform the anchor beyond what difficulty predicts are flagged as potentially exposed. The secondary signal is \\emph{cross-model agreement}: items receiving uniformly high scores across architecturally diverse models suggest exposure rather than genuine easiness, quantified via $z$-score standardization of the inter-model agreement distribution. These signals are combined as a weighted composite (75\\% anchoring, 25\\% agreement), clipped to $[0, 1]$, and treated as fixed in Stage~3. Where model log-probabilities are accessible, LogProber can provide an additional gray-box memorization signal, though it is not used in our current experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via NUTS \\citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \\S\\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as\n%\n\\begin{align}\n\\label{eq:contam_prior}\n\\sigma_c &\\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\quad c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\nonumber \\\\\nc_n &= c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c,\n\\end{align}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.\n\nConvergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters (exp-012). Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.}\n\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\nAdding a multiplicative term to a measurement model raises the question of whether the new parameters can be separated from the existing ones. We establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identification. All proofs assume the standard location constraint $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]\n\\label{prop:no_new}\nWhen $e_i$ is fixed externally, the gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond that already present in the standard MFRM.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nSuppose $c_n' = c_n + \\delta$ preserves all response probabilities. Then $\\delta \\cdot e_i = 0$ for all items. Since $e_i \\neq 0$ for at least one item by assumption, $\\delta = 0$. The key observation is that fixing $e_i$ externally eliminates the bilinear ambiguity $c_n \\cdot e_i = (\\lambda c_n)(\\lambda^{-1} e_i)$ that would otherwise render the decomposition non-unique.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]\n\\label{prop:exposure_variation}\nIf $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWhen exposure is constant, the linear predictor becomes $(\\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e) - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k$, which is equivalent to a standard MFRM with reparameterized ability $\\theta^*_n = \\theta_n + c_n \\cdot e$. No configuration of response data can distinguish $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ from $(\\theta_n + \\epsilon, c_n - \\epsilon/e)$ for any $\\epsilon$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{corollary}\n$\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is a necessary condition for identifiability of $c_n$.\n\\end{corollary}\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]\n\\label{thm:sufficient}\nThe G-MFRM is locally identifiable, up to the standard location constraint, if the following conditions hold: (i) a connected observation design (each item is observed by at least one model--judge pair), (ii) $J \\geq 2$ judges, (iii) for each model $n$, the set of observed items spans at least two distinct exposure values ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ over observed items), and (iv) standard normalization $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof, given in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}, follows \\citet{rothenberg1971}: non-singularity of the Fisher information matrix is established via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix determinant and a Schur complement argument for the remaining blocks. Conditions~(i) and~(ii) are standard multi-rater design requirements, satisfied by the connected design in our evaluation (44.7\\% fill rate; \\S\\ref{sec:setup}); condition~(iii) is the substantive constraint that each model's observed items include varying exposure levels.\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop}\n\nThe staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \\citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \\citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.\n\nLet $\\hat{e}_i = e_i + \\epsilon_i$ with $\\epsilon_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:attenuation}\n\\lambda(\\sigma) = \\frac{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \\sigma^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso attenuation reaches 50\\% when $\\sigma = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic DGP ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \\approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\\sigma \\approx 0.2$.\\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop} provides empirical validation that $\\hat{\\theta}$ estimates remain robust under such departures (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} shows the empirical degradation across noise levels (see also Figure~\\ref{fig:error_prop} in the Appendix). Below the critical threshold ($\\sigma \\leq 0.2$), degradation is mild: $\\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\\% (from $0.128 \\pm 0.018$ to $0.135 \\pm 0.018$) and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\\% (from $0.533 \\pm 0.187$ to $0.575 \\pm 0.198$). At $\\sigma = 0.1$, the increases are only 1.4\\% and 2.3\\% respectively. Beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe: $\\theta$-RMSE rises 36.9\\% and $c_n$-RMSE rises 57.9\\%. Contamination estimates $c_n$ are more sensitive than ability estimates $\\theta_n$ because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$ (multiplicative coupling), while $\\theta_n$ contributes additively and is anchored by all observations regardless of exposure.\n\nThese bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \\citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability.\n\n\\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:fair_scores}\n\nThe Stage~3 posterior mean of $\\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.",

      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/background.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Confounding in LLM Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:confounding}\n\nThe Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) extends classical Rasch measurement to settings with multiple rater facets \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm}. Originating in educational measurement, the model treats rater variation as a nuisance facet to be estimated and removed rather than a source of noise to be averaged over. In the LLM evaluation context, where $J$ judges score $N$ models across $I$ items, the MFRM specifies\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\tau_k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\tau_k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $Y_{nij}$ is the ordinal rating by judge $j$ for model $n$ on item $i$, $\\theta_n$ is the ability of model $n$ ($n = 1, \\ldots, N$), $b_i$ is the difficulty of item $i$ ($i = 1, \\ldots, I$), $d_j$ is the severity of judge $j$ ($j = 1, \\ldots, J$), and $\\tau_k$ is the threshold for category $k$. All facets reside on a common logit scale, so model ability is directly comparable after adjustment for item difficulty and judge severity. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} applied this framework to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, showing that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects in multi-judge panels.\n\nHowever, standard MFRM assumes that all systematic sources of score variation are captured by the modeled facets. This assumption breaks down when some models have been exposed to benchmark items during training \\citep{dcr2025}. Such contamination inflates scores on exposed items through memorization rather than genuine ability. We represent this effect as an interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ is the contamination susceptibility of model $n$ and $e_i \\in [0, 1]$ is the exposure level of item $i$. The complete data-generating process becomes\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:full}\n\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nWhen a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$, $\\tilde{b}_i$, and $\\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.\n\nThe consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.\n\n\\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]\n\\label{obs:ovb}\nLet the full model be $\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\\tilde{\\eta}_{nijk} = \\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\tilde{b}_i - \\tilde{d}_j - \\tau_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \\citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:ovb}\n\\mathrm{plim}(\\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\theta_n) = \\gamma_n \\cdot c_n,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\\, c_n \\cdot e_i) \\neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{observation}\n\nThe linear approximation in Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb} holds when $c_n \\cdot e_i$ is small relative to the logit scale; for large contamination effects, the gated model (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}) captures the full nonlinear interaction without relying on this approximation. The bias combines a specification component from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$, which persists regardless of confounding, with a confounding component from non-orthogonality between $d_j$ and $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\\rho \\in \\{0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9\\}$, indicating that the omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}).\n\nThis confounding undermines both sequential estimation strategies. A bias-first approach, as in CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, calibrates $d_j$ before estimating ability, but the resulting $\\hat{\\theta}$ is already inflated for contaminated models because the omitted $c_n \\cdot e_i$ term has been absorbed into the ability estimates. A contamination-first approach, as in DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, adjusts for contamination before addressing judge effects, but judge severity is then misattributed as contamination signal. Synthetic validation confirms this failure: with $N = 50$ models, $I = 100$ items, $J = 8$ judges, and 20 random seeds, the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$). As illustrated in Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}, the bias is not random but directional: contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_ovb_bias}\n\\caption{Sequential estimation bias ($\\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{seq}} - \\theta_{\\mathrm{true}}$) versus true contamination susceptibility $c_n$. Pearson $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$) confirms systematic overestimation of contaminated models. Synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds).}\n\\label{fig:ovb_bias}\n\\end{figure}",
        "numLines": 43,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 43
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "e83fa794-6288-4edc-bc8e-68cb531ce3fa",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge Language Model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on LLM-as-a-judge protocols, where one or more LLM judges score model responses on structured benchmarks \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}. Two well-documented sources of distortion affect these evaluations: \\emph{judge bias}, where different judges assign systematically different scores to the same response, and \\emph{benchmark contamination}, where models achieve inflated scores on items encountered during training. Both problems have received sustained research attention, producing separate toolkits for bias calibration and contamination detection. These toolkits treat the two distortions as independent phenomena, a separation that reflects their origins in different research communities rather than any principled argument for independence. Accurate ability ranking is essential for model selection, safety evaluation, and benchmark design; systematic distortion in estimated abilities undermines all three. Yet a practitioner who needs both corrections must apply them sequentially: first correct one source of distortion, then the other. This sequential approach produces unreliable ability estimates. In synthetic experiments with 50 models, 100 items, and 8 judges, sequential estimation yields ability root mean squared error (RMSE) of $0.254 \\pm 0.045$, while joint estimation achieves $0.124 \\pm 0.016$, a 51.2\\% reduction (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). The gap does not arise from estimation noise or algorithmic limitations of individual methods; it reflects structural confounding between judge bias and contamination that no sequential pipeline can resolve.\n\nExisting methods address these distortions in isolation. Judge bias corrections---CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, UDA \\citep{uda2026}---remove systematic inter-judge differences without modeling contamination. Contamination detection tools---ConStat \\citep{constat2024}, DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, LogProber \\citep{logprober2024}---adjust for memorization without accounting for judge effects (see Section~\\ref{sec:related_work} for detailed review). A bias-first pipeline absorbs contamination-induced score inflation into ability estimates, because the bias model attributes all residual systematic variation to genuine ability. A contamination-first pipeline misattributes judge severity differences as contamination, because score elevation from a lenient judge is indistinguishable from elevation due to memorization without a joint model. In either ordering, the first correction stage produces estimates distorted by the unmodeled facet, and the second stage inherits this distortion.\n\nWe formalize this confounding as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem \\citep{angrist2009mostly, chernozhukov2022ovb}. Within the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) framework \\citep{linacre1989mfrm}, the complete data-generating process includes both judge severity $d_j$ and a contamination interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Fitting a specification that omits the contamination term produces biased parameter estimates: the omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$ induces a specification bias that persists regardless of confounding structure, with an additional directional component when judge severity and contamination are non-orthogonal. The OVB framework predicts that the bias magnitude scales with the contamination susceptibility $c_n$ of each model, so the most contaminated models receive the largest upward distortion in estimated ability. For practical benchmark evaluations, this means that the more a model's training data overlaps with the benchmark, the more its ability is overestimated, precisely the failure mode that contamination correction is designed to prevent. Synthetic validation confirms the predicted pattern: the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), which shows that contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\nWe propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which combines the multi-facet structure of MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, jiao2025mfrm_llm} with the gating mechanism of Doubly-Gated Item Response Theory (DG-IRT) \\citep{shu2013dgirt} to jointly estimate model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility in a single measurement framework. G-MFRM extends the MFRM linear predictor with a multiplicative contamination gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ captures model-specific contamination susceptibility and $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies per-item exposure. When $c_n = 0$ or $e_i = 0$, the gating term vanishes and the model reduces to standard MFRM, so uncontaminated model-item pairs incur no distortion. Because the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ cannot be uniquely decomposed from response data alone, G-MFRM employs a three-stage calibration procedure: (1) standard MFRM warm-start to obtain initial parameter estimates, (2) external per-item exposure estimation from evidence independent of the response data, and (3) joint Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling with $e_i$ fixed from Stage~2. We provide a formal identifiability analysis (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) that proves fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). A complementary error propagation analysis bounds the impact of Stage~2 measurement noise: when exposure estimation error satisfies $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, ability RMSE degradation remains below 6\\%. Figure~\\ref{fig:framework} provides an overview of the complete framework.\n\nWe begin by formalizing the confounding between judge severity and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias within the MFRM framework (\\S\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}), motivating the joint calibration approach. Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}",
        "numLines": 17,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 17
      }
    },
