      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (top-4 models' 95\\% CIs exclude zero, though posterior CI coverage is 71\\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation confirms improved predictive fit for G-MFRM over standard MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = -60$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$).\n\\end{abstract}",
        "numLines": 3,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 3
      }
    },

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for full coverage across all judges and target models, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (5{,}661 observations; 44.7\% fill rate of the full $N \times I \times J$ tensor). Eight target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \citep{zheng2023mtbench}.
8	
9	\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score all model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}).
10	
11	\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\S\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).
12	
13	\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro NUTS with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ and bulk/tail ESS \citep{vehtari2021rhat}.
14	
15	\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}
16	\label{sec:main_results}
17	
18	We compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\tau$ and Spearman $\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\small
23	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
24	\toprule
25	\textbf{Method} & $\tau$ & $\rho$ \\
26	\midrule
27	Raw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
28	MFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
29	Sequential & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
30	CyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
31	CyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
32	DCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
33	Pref.\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 \\
34	\midrule
35	G-MFRM (ours) & \textbf{0.857} & \textbf{0.952} \\
36	\bottomrule
37	\end{tabular}
38	\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (8 target models, 9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero).}
39	\label{tab:main_results}
40	\end{table}
41	
42	All seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\tau = 0.786$, $\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\tau$ takes only ${\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$, $\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\Delta\tau$ 95\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\Delta\tau \geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\tau = 0.922$ vs.\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\theta_{\text{mini}} > \theta_{\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$, $\theta$-difference 95\% CI $[-0.227, +0.722]$).
43	
44	While the bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides decisive evidence that the contamination gating term captures genuine data structure: G-MFRM achieves $\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$, far exceeding the threshold of 2.0 for decisive model preference; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. The contamination parameters improve out-of-sample prediction, not merely in-sample fit.
45	
46	The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\hat{\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\bar{c} \approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\hat{\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \geq 2$, with $\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_count}).
47	
48	\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}
49	\label{sec:error_prop_results}
50	
51	Section~\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\sigma$ remains below $\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).
52	
53	\begin{table}[t]
54	\centering
55	\small
56	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
57	\toprule
58	$\sigma$ & $\theta$-RMSE & $\Delta\theta$\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\Delta c_n$\% \\
59	\midrule
60	0.0 & 0.128 $\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\
61	0.1 & 0.130 $\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\
62	0.2 & 0.135 $\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\
63	0.5 & 0.175 $\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\
64	\bottomrule
65	\end{tabular}
66	\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\% when exposure noise $\sigma \leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}
67	\label{tab:error_prop}
68	\end{table}
69	
70	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\%; beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial ($+$36.9\% and $+$57.9\%). Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\sigma = 0.5$ ($\tau > 0.88$).
71	
72	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} (Appendix) reports ablation results on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). The dominant effect is joint versus staged estimation: staged estimation degrades $\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\times$ ($\tau$ drops 8.3pp, $p < 0.001$), directly validating the OVB analysis; continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\% over binary gating ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ does not affect rankings ($\tau$ identical, $p = 0.34$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\%, $p < 0.001$), confirming its role as a diagnostic component.
73	
```

_source line 3237 · 2026-05-23T06:51:04.874Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01A1bQeKKnrgBwg35cmhNcQC`</summary>

```text
1	We identify five limitations. First, although Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\Delta\tau = 0.071$ at $\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \approx 17$ models for 80\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage is 71\% (50 Monte Carlo seeds), below the 95\% nominal level \citep{talts2018sbc}, indicating moderate posterior overconfidence likely due to the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint and NUTS tree depth saturation. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected, but reported CIs should be interpreted as lower bounds on true uncertainty. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted.
2	
3	Code and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL].
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Lwh2FQrXuoeQAEmFjmdFpW`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}
5	CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias through round-robin judge assignment but treats residual score variation as genuine ability. UDA \citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor adjustment reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\%, though the correction is a black box with no contamination mechanism. \citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability through $J/\Delta J$ diagnostics, identifying sign-reversal failure modes. All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Contamination Detection and Adjustment.}
8	ConStat \citep{constat2024} estimates contamination via reference model comparison ($r^2 = 0.94$), establishing the framework G-MFRM's Stage~2 builds upon. DCR \citep{dcr2025} adjusts scores through fuzzy inference rules; LogProber \citep{logprober2024} detects memorization from token-level log-probabilities; Min-K\% \citep{mink2024} uses perplexity signals. All assume a fixed rater, so judge severity can masquerade as contamination. \citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side contamination (same-family leakage $\approx$ 8.9\%), complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus.
9	
10	\paragraph{Contamination Avoidance.}
11	LiveBench \citep{livebench2025} and TRUCE \citep{truce2024} prevent contamination by design through frequently updated items and confidential computing, respectively. These are complementary: avoidance reduces exposed items, while G-MFRM corrects for residual contamination.
12	
13	\paragraph{IRT and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}
14	\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to LLM raters without contamination terms. \citet{uto2020generalized} extend MFRM with Bayesian HMC estimation. \citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose judge reliability via Graded Response Models, and PSN-IRT \citep{zhou2025psnirt} extends IRT to neural architectures---none include contamination facets. The closest antecedent, DG-IRT \citep{shu2013dgirt}, is compared in Section~\ref{sec:model_spec}. G-MFRM bridges these threads by jointly modeling judge bias and contamination (\S\ref{sec:confounding}).
```

</details>

          " All seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\\tau$ takes only ${\\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$, $\\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\\Delta\\tau \\geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\\theta_{\\text{mini}} > \\theta_{\\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$, $\\theta$-difference 95\\% CI $[-0.227, +0.722]$).",
          " ",
          "-While the bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides decisive evidence that the contamination gating term captures genuine data structure: G-MFRM achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$, far exceeding the threshold of 2.0 for decisive model preference; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. $\\Delta$ELPD measures predictive fit, not ranking accuracy; the ranking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) remains non-significant at $N = 8$ (Section~\\ref{sec:setup}).",

          " The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",

          " While the bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at $N = 8$, LOO cross-validation provides strong evidence for improved predictive fit: G-MFRM achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$ (SE $= 9.4$; $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable estimates. $\\Delta$ELPD measures predictive fit, not ranking accuracy; the ranking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) remains non-significant at $N = 8$ (Section~\\ref{sec:setup}).",
          " ",
          "-The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to the moderate posterior overconfidence observed in synthetic CI coverage (71\\%; see Limitations). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap, and the global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",
          "+The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a significant gradient by model age. Four models have 95\\% CIs entirely above 1.0: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($5.60$ [4.40, 6.80]), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$ [3.10, 5.30]), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$ [1.40, 4.60]), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$ [1.10, 3.90]). The most recent models show weak contamination with CIs near zero: GPT-4o ($0.88$ [0.05, 2.20]) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$ [0.06, 2.20]). The top-two and bottom-two CIs do not overlap (reported CIs are approximate lower bounds on true uncertainty given empirical coverage of ${\\sim}$71\\%; see Limitations), and the global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ [1.70, 3.40] confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).     "originalFile": "We identify five limitations. First, although Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ at $\\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \\approx 17$ models for 80\\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage is 71\\% (50 Monte Carlo seeds), below the 95\\% nominal level \\citep{talts2018sbc}, indicating moderate posterior overconfidence likely due to the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint and NUTS tree depth saturation. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected, but reported CIs should be interpreted as lower bounds on true uncertainty. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted.\n\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL].\n",

Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ at $\\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \\approx 17$ models for 80\\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage is 71\\% (50 Monte Carlo seeds), below the 95\\% nominal level \\citep{talts2018sbc}, indicating moderate posterior overconfidence likely due to the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint and NUTS tree depth saturation. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected, but reported CIs should be interpreted as lower bounds on true uncertainty. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted.",
          "+We identify six limitations. First, although Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ at $\\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \\approx 17$ models for 80\\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage is 71\\% (50 Monte Carlo seeds), below the 95\\% nominal level \\citep{talts2018sbc}, indicating moderate posterior overconfidence likely due to the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint and NUTS tree depth saturation. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected, but reported CIs should be interpreted as lower bounds on true uncertainty. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted. Sixth, the bilinear contamination term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ assumes rank-1 structure---a single latent dimension of contamination susceptibility per model. In practice, contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\\ reasoning items), requiring a higher-rank decomposition $\\sum_r c_{nr} \\cdot e_{ir}$ that the current model does not capture.",
          " ",
          " Code and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL]."
        ]
      }
    ],

All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}\nCyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias through round-robin judge assignment but treats residual score variation as genuine ability. UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor adjustment reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\%, though the correction is a black box with no contamination mechanism. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability through $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics, identifying sign-reversal failure modes. All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection and Adjustment.}\nConStat \\citep{constat2024} estimates contamination via reference model comparison ($r^2 = 0.94$), establishing the framework G-MFRM's Stage~2 builds upon. DCR \\citep{dcr2025} adjusts scores through fuzzy inference rules; LogProber \\citep{logprober2024} detects memorization from token-level log-probabilities; Min-K\\% \\citep{mink2024} uses perplexity signals. All assume a fixed rater, so judge severity can masquerade as contamination. \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side contamination (same-family leakage $\\approx$ 8.9\\%), complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Avoidance.}\nLiveBench \\citep{livebench2025} and TRUCE \\citep{truce2024} prevent contamination by design through frequently updated items and confidential computing, respectively. These are complementary: avoidance reduces exposed items, while G-MFRM corrects for residual contamination.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\n\\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to LLM raters without contamination terms. \\citet{uto2020generalized} extend MFRM with Bayesian HMC estimation. \\citet{choi2026irt_judge} diagnose judge reliability via Graded Response Models, and PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2025psnirt} extends IRT to neural architectures---none include contamination facets. The closest antecedent, DG-IRT \\citep{shu2013dgirt}, is compared in Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec}. G-MFRM bridges these threads by jointly modeling judge bias and contamination (\\S\\ref{sec:confounding}).",
